ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-13-senat-52-2.u1	I'm reopening the session. We return to the examination of the first item of the agenda: the Act amending the Act on the National Judicial Council and certain other laws. He's an ombudsperson. Do you, Mr. Ombudsman, wish to speak? [[Ombudsman Adam Bodnar: Yes.]] Yes? You're welcome.
ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-13-senat-52-2.u2	Dear Marshal, Dear Minister, Dear Minister, High Izbo! I would like to thank you very much for the opportunity to speak in the Senate on the Act on the National Judicial Council. The Ombudsman participates in the debate on judicial reform because I believe that both the Law on the National Judicial Council and the Law on the Supreme Court, as well as the previously adopted law on the system of universal courts, are of fundamental importance for the implementation of the constitutional right to a court as set out in Article. 45 Constitutions and Art. 6 the European Convention on Human Rights. In addition, I would like to stress that the concerns expressed by numerous NGOs, numerous international organisations, including the National Judicial Council Act, are concerned about the proposed laws. I believe that this concern is justified because the entry into force of the Act on the National Judicial Council, and perhaps the Law on the Supreme Court, will have very concrete consequences for the independence of the judiciary. I believe that the constitutional right to court cannot be exercised if the courts are not independent. This independence of the court is the foundation on which the principle of three divisions of power is based. Moreover, if we observe the process, and this process can be observed, concentrating the various powers of power in the hands of the police, the prosecutor's office, the special services, the courts are the only shield for citizens, the courts are the only shield against the sovereignty of the executive authority and should constitute this defence of civil rights and freedoms. But it's hard to make that possible without their independence. Dear Marshal, High Izbo! I would like to begin my speech with a reminder, and today it serves well, what were the historical grounds for the creation of the National Judicial Council, and why the National Judicial Council is shaped in such a way and not in any other way on the basis of the Constitution. I would like to say this because the approach to this issue is important from the point of view of how we think about who should appoint members of the judiciary or members of the judges of the National Judicial Council, which is basically the main point of the dispute as regards the law on the National Judicial Council. The first such historic moment in terms of the legacy of the "Solidarity," when serious discussions were held about guaranteeing the independence of the judiciary, was the announcement of Letter 59 of 1975, letter 59 of the signatories, which was addressed to the authorities. It was then written that the independence of the courts from the executive authorities should be ensured. Later, demands on the independence of the judiciary appeared in the work of the Workers' Defence Committee. As far as the National Judicial Council itself is concerned, the most important point in the discussion on the subject was the nationwide forum of judicial staff, which took place on 17 January 1981 on the initiative of the judge of the District Court in Krakow Kazimierz Barczyk at the Jagiellonian University. This was the moment when it was the forum and then the initiatives that were taken under, you can say, the leadership of Judge Kazimierz Barczyk... This was the moment when the Civil Service Centre of Legislative Initiatives was established, and this was the first time that a request was made to set up a National Judicial Council. And in fact, what was undisputed later throughout the 1980s is that judicial reforms should be based on the independence of the judiciary, on the preservation of the three divisions of power, on the integrity of judges, but also on the creation of this independent National Judicial Council. This process and the fact that this postulate was basically undisputed and not subject to more discussion, were described very well by Professor Andrzej Friszke in his paper at the Round Table. About Poland The democratic rule of law. This paper was delivered on 3 September this year at a conference at the University of Warsaw. Well, throughout the 1980s, it seems like it's worth emphasising this request that was still supported until the Round Table was held. The Round Table began on 6 February 1989. Representatives of the communist authorities and representatives of the "Solidarity" sub-group were involved in the work of the legal and judicial reform. The team was chaired, as we all know, by Professor Adam Strzembosz and Łukasz Balcer, and the opposition side was created by outstanding activists, including others. Zbigniew Bujak, Lech Falandysz, Jarosław Kaczyński, Zbigniew Romaszewski, Jadwiga Skórzewska, Adam Strzembosz just, Zofia Wasilkowska. And it is very important that already at the beginning of the Round Table's work there was a proposal to create a Supreme Judicial Council. I would like to emphasise here. These are the words of Mrs Jadwiga Skórzewska It was said that the formation of a chief judicial council consisting of delegates of general court assemblies, Supreme Administrative Courts, Supreme Courts and delegates of parliamentary clubs was being established. Please note that the word "delegates" is of fundamental importance. Since the beginning of the work, it has been thought of the National Judicial Council in this way as an organ which will constitute a delegation of judges from the various general assemblies. It can be said that the historical date of this decision within the framework of this subtable was 22 February 1989. It was then that the legal and judicial reform sub-group decided to create the highest judicial council. This was justified by Jerzy Ciemniewski, and Jarosław Kaczyński supported this position during the work. It can be said that there was no discussion that the National Judicial Council, then known as the Supreme Judicial Council, was to be established. There were only disputes about naming. The bone of disagreement, a matter on which the representatives of the communist authorities and of Solidarity did not agree, was a general reform of criminal policy. In this paper, Professor Friszke points out that this was a failure, because various issues concerning the reform of the criminal procedure have not been met. But the institutional issues have been agreed. And if we now look at the agreement of 5 April 1989, that is, the agreement that summarized the political arrangements, we will find there a passage: "Judge's independence will be guarded by the National Council of Courts, consisting mostly of judges delegated by the General Assembly of the Supreme Court, the Supreme Administrative Court and universal courts. It will present to the President the appointment of a judge or promotion to a higher court of one of the two candidates submitted by the General Assembly of the courts of the district where the need for the appointment of judges arose. The independence of the judiciary will be based on the principle of incapacity of judges (except in the cases set out in the Act) and the failure of judges, contrary to their will, to another place of office. I stress that the word "delegated" is very important, because this is our tradition of government, historical tradition. The National Judicial Council is created in such a way that, on the one hand, there is a political representation consisting of 4 Members, 2 Senators, a representative of the President and the Prosecutor General, and on the other hand, there are judges posted by the relevant judicial assemblies. And if we look at our 1997 constitution now, we'll see that it reflects this historic character of the council. No one questioned this, and even if there were critical voices in doctrine, I do not recall any serious arguments suggesting that this is precisely where the National Judicial Council should be reformed, that this provision of the article. 187 of the Constitution should be amended in such a way as to give the opportunity to appoint members of the National Judicial Council to Parliament. And I would like to draw attention to that, because I think that since we have such a wonderful and great historical heritage, the heritage resulting from the fact that in communist times human rights were violated, that the courts were subject to political power and mechanisms were created to counteract it... The National Judicial Council was supposed to be one of those mechanisms. Its construction has just assumed this balancing of different types of authorities, it can be said, the disassembly of the National Judicial Council between the different authorities, so that it is the body of control and mutual balancing of the authorities. And I don't normally see any arguments why this should be ruined, and I don't see any arguments why this should be changed. When I hear the argument that more political control is needed... In my view, it is rather important to ensure that Members, Senators and the President's representative make maximum use of their capacity to act in the National Judicial Council, but that Parliament is not given the opportunity to appoint judges. And now what may raise doubts in this context, and what was already debated yesterday. We must be aware that if Members are to choose the members of the National Judicial Council, those members of the judiciary, the very situation in which the judge becomes the nominator of one party or another, of one or another parliamentary club, he already creates, you can say, a threat to his sense of independence and probably to the public's acceptance. From this, it seems to me, it is worth to begin the discussion at all: that the procedure for nomination and support by one or another political party already raises doubts. However, we must also be aware that, after all, the procedure laid down in the Act allows, at least, the members of the National Council of Courts to become judges posted to the Ministry of Justice. There is no obstacle to these judges, on the one hand, being candidates and on the other hand being candidates for the National Judicial Council. If we look also at the numbers, i.e. the fact that about 200 judges work at the Ministry of Justice, has a kind of relationship, as well as a service relationship, with the Minister of Justice, then we will see that the danger is serious. Moreover, the entry into force of the law on the system of common courts has led some judges to have a direct relationship with the Minister of Justice also because they became Presidents of the courts. Here I would like to stress that the law on the system of common courts allows for the dismissal of the Presidents of the courts practically on the basis of a discretionary decision of the Minister and also the appointment of new Presidents of the courts in the same way. And that also creates, you can say, a relationship that already in itself threatens the independence of the judiciary. And let's imagine now that it's just like this. These newly appointed Presidents will be designated as candidates for the National Judicial Council. So that citizens may feel that this body will not be fully independent. I draw such scenarios to show that these are issues that have a very specific, specific meaning in terms of body design, and that rules that seemingly sound neutral can serve to achieve certain objectives. So I would like to stress that the issue that raises the greatest doubts is that Parliament will have an impact on the cast of the National Judicial Council, and in this way what is, can be said, will be destroyed by the acquis of our history, the history of the eighties and later years, which is reflected in the 1997 Constitution. Of course, it is worth mentioning that this type of construction also raises concerns from the point of view of international standards. If we invoke one of these standards Kiev's principles concerning the independence of the judiciary, which have been created in the framework of the work of the Organisation for Security and Cooperation in Europe... These principles provide that if a body is set up to safeguard the independence of the judiciary, such as the National Judicial Council The majority of its members should be appointed by the judges themselves, because this is the form ensure the independence of that body. And I would like to stress that, for some reason, this legislative reform is of interest to the international community. The OSCE prepared its own critical opinion, which was presented at the meeting of the Committee on Human Rights, the rule of law and the Petitions of the Senate on Monday, but I would also like to draw attention to the opinion which emerged from the UN Special Rapporteur on the independence of the judiciary. The UN Special Rapporteur is a body that is appointed by the UN Human Rights Council and is tasked with observing the development of standards in different countries. He made an official visit to Poland a few weeks ago and after this visit he made general recommendations, which also address the issue of independence of the National Judicial Council. He pointed out two issues: firstly, that a law in this form could cause concern in terms of threats to the independence of the judiciary; secondly, to shorten the term of office of the members of the National Judicial Council. A similar concern was expressed by the Venice Commission in its opinion, which was adopted on 8 December this year. On that day, the Venice Commission adopted 2 reports, 2 reports on Polish legislation. One referred to the independence of the judiciary, the other referred to the reform of the prosecutor's office. Now I would like to refer precisely to this second report, the report by the Venice Commission on judicial reform, because I think that... listening to the debate yesterday, I thought it was worth explaining one thing and filling it in. The Venice Commission, in preparing its opinion, was based on the draft law, which was, you might say, prepared earlier by the President, on this original project. That is, on this project, which assumed that Parliament would choose 3/5 of the composition of the National Judicial Council and then, in the absence of agreement on the choice, the introduction of the principle: 1 Member is 1 vote. As we know, for various reasons, this solution has been abandoned. The solution that is currently in the Act is the solution that involves the appointment of members by an absolute majority in the absence of a choice of 3/5. In fact, in this respect, the opinion of the Venice Commission, as Minister Surówka-Pasek said, is obsolete because it does not address the amended issue. Nevertheless, I would like to stress that the Venice Commission points out that already entrusting and changing the way in which Parliament is appointed threatens the independence of the judiciary. And what has not changed with regard to the original project and the present one, that is what is of second concern to me and should be of concern to any person who is looking at the legislation, i.e. shortening the term of office of members of the National Judicial Council. This is the introduction of a solution that shortens the current term of office of the members of the National Judicial Council for the introduction of this, one can say, a common term for all members. The Venice Commission refers to this issue in a very direct way and devotes as many as 3 paragraphs of its opinion to this issue. First of all, the Venice Commission points out that the mere introduction of this common term may raise doubts as unsynchronised terms are a common feature in the collegial bodies in Europe. Why? Because they guarantee institutional memory and continuation of the work of such bodies. Moreover, they ensure internal pluralism and strengthen the independence of such bodies. The Venice Commission points out that if persons are appointed for different terms of office, this can ensure pluralism of their opinions. The Venice Commission indicates And here I would like to quote Even if we assume that the joint term of office is politically justified, this objective can be otherwise achieved in a way that does not affect the current term of office of the members. The judgment of the Constitutional Tribunal of June 2017 does not call for the removal of all current members of the National Judicial Council. The current members should remain in office until the end of the term of office for which they have been appointed, while new members may be appointed for a shorter period of time. With the assurance that at some point the objective of the joint term of office of all members will be achieved. As the Venice Commission points out, this solution will not only respect the security of the term of office but will also ensure a better institutional continuation of the work of this type of body. I would like to point out that the role of the Venice Commission is not merely criticism. Its role is, in a sense, what we need to be aware of, advising countries. After all, the Venice Commission was set up as a body of the Council of Europe The body equipped, one can say, with the greatest authority in relation to the opinion of the various laws, proposals for amendments, even the constitution; the body consisting of many prominent experts in constitutional law. This body, you can say, extends the limits of its action beyond the Council of Europe. The States that support the Venice Commission are in total 61 together with the United States and Canada. It is hard to imagine another body that enjoys greater authority in the world. Since there is an indication, the suggestion that a solution to this common term could have been reached in a different way, while not violating standards or constitutions, it seems to me that such a recommendation should be taken to heart. I would like to point out that these are issues that should not be dealt with in a hasty manner. These are issues that should be the subject of wider consultation. It seems to me that this indication that a political objective can be achieved in the form of a joint term of office by allowing the former members of the National Judicial Council to complete their term of office is worth more consideration. At the end of my speech, I would like to point out that we have two types of arguments in favour of not reforming the National Judicial Council in this way. First of all, historical arguments. It is our historical heritage that the National Council of Courts works in this way, not in any other way and that it represents the representation of judges . through the right of appointment 15 members of the National Judicial Council And the representation of other types of power. This is a construction that for many, many years has been And I think it's valued in the world. I recall that when two years ago, in June 2015, I took part in a conference in Brno, where the creation of a national judicial council was considered, I was asked to present a Polish model because it was considered that the Polish model was very interesting when it comes to creating these good relations between the various authorities. The second argument is a constitutional argument. I think it's Art. 187 should be interpreted in such a way that it is the judges who appoint these 15 members of the National Courts Council and that this systematic and historical interpretation of the Constitution should lead to this. Secondly, I believe that the legislator should not shorten the term of office of the constitutional authorities. If such practices arise that the legislator decides to shorten his term of office, someone may ask themselves: who will be next? What next constitutional authority will have a shortened term of office by law? And with this question, I would like to conclude my speech. Thank you. [[Applause]]

ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-13-senat-52-2.u12	Thank you so much for asking. To answer Senator Fedorovich's question, I will explain that there are generally two models of appointing judges in Europe. One model is a model: a judge's position as a crown of a legal career, i.e. a model in which he chooses, selects already outstanding, experienced lawyers, having absolute trust in them, and then nominates them to the courts. This is also possible in Poland, although it is relatively rarely used, which I think should be regretted. The President of the Criminal Chamber present here, Judge Stanisław Zabłocki, is an example of this because his appointment to the Supreme Court was preceded by a career of attorney, a prominent lawyer. The second model is based on the so-called. a judge's career and assumes that lawyers are trained and prepared for the role of judge, prepared for how to exercise judicial authority. And this is a model that, in fact, seems to be currently dominant in Europe because justice requires quite special preparation, as well as quite large judges. And the model in which the judge's position is the crown of the legal professions is not fully implemented. However, regardless of the model, whether one or another, in all the procedures that are created, it is always placed on this far-reaching independence of the judge at the time of appointment, in such a way that this judge is not subject to any political pressure. The National Judicial Council, such bodies, plays a very important role in the selection process of candidates, the process of verifying their qualifications and subsequently establishing them, or the presentation to the relevant authorities, such as the President, of their appointments. With regard to the possible impact of politicians on the sense of independence, we have a Polish case that was discussed yesterday. It is a matter of two Mr Urban v. Poland, Mr. Counselor Silchoń was a proxy in this case. I'm sure it wasn't Mr. Jerzy Urban, he had other stories before the Strasbourg tribunal, they were just two gentlemen. The European Court of Human Rights has pointed out that this, rather than a different design concerning the appointment of judicial assistants, could put their judicial independence at risk. In this context, you can say yes. What we need to be aware of is that if we lower the standards concerning nomination procedures, including the standard concerning the independence of the National Council of Courts, unfortunately, at some point there may be a charge that judges who are appointed in Poland are not independent. So it can happen that this will not only have consequences in the context of the judgments of the European Court of Human Rights, because even if it occurs, it is a perspective quite distant over time, but it may also be dangerous to challenge national courts judgments precisely because of issues relating to judicial independence. I am referring specifically to cooperation in civil matters, in criminal matters. We can imagine, for example. a court in France which states that because the judgment was delivered by a court in respect of which we have doubts as to its independence In view of the various factors, since the President of this court was appointed directly by the Minister, since the nomination procedures were not entirely clear and precise and the National Council of the Court of Justice reduced its level of independence We refuse to cooperate with our courts, e.g. in the case of a European arrest warrant. So that's a very serious perspective. I recall the opinions of the Venice Commission, because the world looks at these issues, looks at these issues and takes this into account. I'd like to give you an example of one very specific case and show you what this might look like. In this example we have been able to challenge doubts, but it is, I think, rather illustrative. There is a case of a lawsuit against ZDF television by Mr.Karol Tendera for the use of the wording of the Polish concentration camp. In this case, the district and appeal courts in Cracow recognised and ruled that the use of such wording in relation to the former Auschwitz prisoner constitutes a violation of his personal property, and the court ordered an apology to Mr. Karol Tendera. I am very lucky to have joined this case and supported Mr. Charles Tendera's argument. And now we come to the second stage, namely the execution of this sentence. Imagine that before the court in Koblenz, the ZDF television agent began to make such arguments that, since there are doubts about the independence of the judiciary in Poland, he also referred to the whole debate that lasted in Poland in July. It is no longer possible to trust this judgment. I presented to Mr. Charles Tendera my own letter, which was presented in the proceedings, and, happily, the court in Koblenz, one can say, did not find this argument concerning the lack of independence of the courts. But this shows a certain perspective, i.e. that such arguments will be used and debated in a moment... not even a debate, will be a way of undermining the credibility of our justice system. Therefore, the independence of the National Judicial Council is so important, the independence of the Supreme Court is so important. As regards the question of shortening the term of office of constitutional authorities, I would like to give two examples, just examples from Hungary. One example is well known, this is the case of Mr András Baki, the President of the Hungarian Supreme Court, whose term of office has been shortened due to the creation of a new body, Kúria. This body was replaced by the Supreme Court, but had virtually the same possibilities of action. The European Court of Human Rights stated that this constitutes an infringement of the law, Article. 6 and Article 6 of Regulation (EU) No 182/2011 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 16 February 2011 laying down the rules and general principles concerning mechanisms for control by Member States of the Commission's exercise of implementing powers (OJ L 347, 20.12.2013, p. 10 The European Convention on Human Rights, i.e. the standards of independence of courts and freedom of expression. It's one of those cases. The second case does not concern a constitutional body, but, I think, a body with a rather similar... body in some sense independent This was the result of the reform of the data protection authority in Hungary, which resulted in a combination of the various institutions that dealt with these issues. And another result was the shortening of András Jóri's term of office, which was, you can say, the Hungarian GIODO, the General Data Protection Officer. And in this case, he was ruling... His term of office was four years, but after 2 years this term was shortened due to the entry into force of the new law which reformed these internal data protection institutions. This case was dealt with by the Court of Justice of the European Union and the Court of Justice of the European Union found that, due to the wording of the article. 7 Charter of Fundamental Rights This is a provision that states that the Union respects the protection of personal data and the independence of the authorities that deal with it. The reform of this kind, which led to the shortening of the term of office, threatened the independence of that body. This is the judgment of the TEU, the Court of Justice of the European Union, in the case of the Commission v Hungary. Because this is where the European Commission initiated the procedure. So that situations like this happen. About Senator Ambrozik's question about the article. 209 The Constitution of the Republic of Poland and the independence and independence of the Ombudsman, I would like to say that our model of the Ombudsman's office is so constructed that the Ombudsman is chosen by one or another political party or representatives of a political party In my case, it was basically a large part of the Civic Platform, several members of PSL and SLD, so that it was a wider representation than what the marshal suggested. But it is referred primarily to as a 5-year term, secondly, to appropriate mechanisms of responsibility, i.e. the presentation of an annual report, and thus this independence and independence are preserved. They are also preserved because this term of office cannot be shortened Unless, for example, a spokesperson. commit a crime or commit other acts that could lead to a specific impeachment procedure. So here... Here I would like to point out that what is the fundamental difference as regards the Office of the Ombudsman and the body of the National Judicial Council, is that the Ombudsman is an authority to speak, comment, to make general speeches, to proceed to proceedings, etc., but the Ombudsman does not take any decisions. Yes? The Ombudsman does not take any decisions which would affect specific decisions on civil rights and freedoms, unlike the National Judicial Council, because the National Judicial Council decides decisions on the professional future of persons, on promotions, on transfer, etc. For example, the recent debate on the problem of judicial assessors shows how important these decisions are. As for Senator Warzocha's question, I wouldn't see a contradiction here, because politicians... However you look at it, the process of making constitutional changes begins with politicians. It is politicians who decide how the system rules are shaped in the country, they prepare the right projects, whether it's the system solutions or the constitutional solutions. As was the case first in 1989 and later as part of the work of the Constitutional Commission of the National Assembly. But what else is the creation of this system framework, and what else is there? The current impact of politicians on the cast of constitutional bodies. If our constitution has provided for such and not another format for the creation of the National Judicial Council, i.e. it has entrusted politicians with the right to appoint 4 Members, 2 Senators and a representative of the President to the National Judicial Council, then the judges should have the right to appoint the others. As far as words are concerned, it can be said, more adjective, but unfortunately I believe that the implementation of the law on the National Judicial Council will worsen the standards on judicial independence. On the other hand, if we look at the law on the National Judicial Council from such a long perspective, i.e., we can say, even since the beginning of the year, we will see that this discussion has been going on for some time, while this last project, which we have been able to get to know, has been presented relatively recently and important amendments have also been introduced in the Committee on Justice and Human Rights. I believe that this will rather put the independence of the judiciary at risk. Now, referring to the question of this second question of Senator Augustine, regarding what ultimately threatens the citizen, I would like to point out, one can say, a quite important distinction in terms of the concept of independence. Because if a citizen comes to court, he has a judge in front of him to settle his case, and this citizen should feel that this judge is absolutely independent in his courts and bases interpretations of laws on the basis of the constitution and laws. Only now there are two concepts, one concept is internal independence, the other concept is external independence. Internal independence is what this judge has in his heart, which is part of his ethos, professional ethics, his, you can say, moral preparation for this function. And you can create a general climate and educate judges to shape this ethos, but this is something very untraceable. This is something that is formed over the years, but also something that depends on how we shape, you can say, the stability of this service. So if someone takes up this function, they know in a way that they're meant to serve those values that they don't care about deep down in their hearts for their whole lives. But there is something else that we call external independence, namely what we create around this judge a circle of different legal safeguards that make us not leave decisions on matters only and only his feelings. Because feelings can be different, they can be feelings of fear, they can be feelings of pressure, feelings of danger to this judge. And these aspects, which are intended to serve external independence, are all aspects of appointing judges, moving them between departments or between courts, irresistibility, but also those that concern how the President of the Court is appointed, how the head of the department is appointed, what role does the judge of the Visitor play, that is, all these professional relations. But this is, of course, also a matter of relation with bodies such as the National Judicial Council. If this body, which is the National Judicial Council, acts in favour of, you can say, the deep independence of the judiciary, and this is what this is for, and not another way of appointing members of the National Judicial Council, then in a sense it strengthens this value in the form of the independence of an external judge. Senator Wach's question about the opinion of the Venice Commission. I would like to draw attention here... I quoted this passage from the opinion on the joint term of office. And here I would like to point out that the committee of §30 opinions starts with words: but even if we assume that a joint term is politically justified, the objective can be achieved in a different way. So the Venice Commission evaluates this, from the point of view of some pragmatism, the rationality of the solution, considering that if the previous experience of committee members and the international experience resulting from the observation of many different systems were taken into account, it would be better if they were divided terms, but if it was considered that such a political decision was taken, this effect could be achieved in a different way, i.e. by varying the expiry of the term of office of individual members and by bringing them all together to the point in which they all begin to fulfil their terms. Yeah, that's what I'm pointing to. But, of course, because of the importance of these systemic arguments, this is the most important argument, this is the cloud of the problem and it is already, you can say, a specific didaxalia compared to what is the core of the regulation. Senator Czarnobaj's question about the procedure for adopting such a law. If there is a systemic change, I believe that the good legislative procedure should look as follows. First, consultations are conducted with the scientific community, the constitutionalist environment through conferences, seminars and, you can say, methods of seeking a certain agreement, understanding of the issue. Later, assumptions are prepared for the draft law, which are the subject of an opinion. The draft law is then prepared, which is the subject of a broad consultation with all possible stakeholders. And only later is this presented as a draft law, there is, of course, an appropriate debate in Parliament, and then there is the President's decision. In this case, I have the impression that these rules have not been respected. If we were looking from the point of view of the ideal lawmaking process, we would see that this perfect, you can say, model was not preserved. And now, to point out that it's possible, I'm going to say that I remember that it was, for example. the draft law on collective actions, the amendment of the law on collective actions, in which the debate was about this way, i.e. there were prior extensive consultations, assumptions for the project, draft law... well, the act has not yet been adopted. But also, as far as the consultation procedure is concerned, in my opinion, it is an addition to this government. I don't hesitate to use that word here. The procedure for preparing the consultation of the reform of higher education by Prime Minister Gowin is the procedure for preparing the consultation of the reform of higher education. Why? Because this is a slightly different model, assuming the selection of scientific consortia, which will consult the scientific community and then discuss the proposals that have been prepared by these consortia. And this, I think, is a very interesting new acquis in terms of how the legislative process can be carried out. But regardless of whether it's one model or another, it all has to go on, because all this has to be prepared for long enough, and it has to be the subject of a reflection that will give all interested parties a chance to think about these changes properly. As regards the issue of political evaluation and this fundamental difference between the Advocate and the Judges, I have already said that the judges are taking very specific decisions, but in my opinion it is not true that judges are not subject to any assessment. Sometimes in our public debate there is such an assessment that judges are irresponsible, not subject to judgment. Ladies and gentlemen, if we look at the judge's assessment, we will first see an in-house assessment, i.e. the correction of judgments by higher courts, including cassation courts. That's the first line. There is an assessment which may result from the visit of the court concerned by the judges of the Visitors who assess the practice in the courts concerned. There is a social evaluation that may result either from the involvement of non-governmental organisations in proceedings, either from the observation of processes, or from the monitoring by the media of how courts work in specific cases. In Poland, the big problem is, in my opinion, that in recent years the profession of court reporters has almost disappeared, and this has always been a very important source of information for all of us about what is happening in courts. Finally, there is a scientific evaluation of the drafting of the vote. The judges really care about how the scientific community evaluates their judgments. There is also a disciplinary assessment, and disciplinary proceedings are, after all, public. Such disciplinary proceedings have repeatedly resulted in very serious consequences for the judges, including removal from the profession. Perhaps the problem is that these other mechanisms don't always work well enough. Perhaps there are not enough NGOs that are interested in justice, not enough journalists who would be interested. Citizens often do not know that they can simply go to court and observe how the court works, that the principle of transparency concerns any citizen who can appear in any court at any time. But I wouldn't agree with the theory that judges aren't subject to any judgment. These are, in my opinion, very serious evaluation mechanisms. And finally, this evaluation. It is also very important that the judge who is doing his job well should be promoted, and should achieve these higher levels. The problem I see here, too, is that sometimes thinking in terms of promotion may give rise to some suspicions about a certain judge's opportunism, but again it is rather... The outside politicians will not solve this, it is the judges themselves who must take care to shape the appropriate mechanisms for verification and promotion of these best, actually wise judges. This is the role of the OSCE, for example, in creating general principles that should be repeated or promoted in other countries. Even if we were to take the example of a country like Denmark or others in which we have a completely different system... Someone might say, "It's just different out there." Only the problem is that in those countries there are no threats to the independence of the judiciary, that the judiciary is treated there as a separate authority. It is particularly important there, which in turn is related to the constitutional culture and tradition of the country. I would like to point out once again that opinion, which was expressed by the OSCE on 5 May, to this original draft law. You could say that things haven't changed much. We read here that judicial councils and other similar bodies are necessary to support and guarantee the independence of the judicial authority in a given country, so they should be independent and impartial on their own, that is, free from interference by the executive and legislative authorities; in fact, interference with the independence of the authorities guaranteeing the independence of the courts could have an impact on the independence of the judiciary as a whole, potentially threatening it. And further: in Poland, the responsibility of such councils generally covers key issues relating to the independence of judges, in particular the appointment of judges and their promotions, and they represent the interests of the judiciary as a whole, in particular in its relations with the executive and legislative authorities. These are the guidelines of the opinion presented by the OSCE. She organized several meetings and created such, you can say, a model of proceedings and recommendations as courts should deal with such judgments Not only at general level but also in the context of individual cases In the sense of how to learn from these judgments and not make similar mistakes in the future. So, of course, this role should continue to be played by the independent National Judicial Council.

Finally, in January 1981 this initiative was set up in the form of a Civil Center for Legislative Initiatives led by Kazimierz Barczyk. The work was attended by judges Stanisław Rudnicki, Zofia Wasilkowska, Kazimierz Barczyk and Adam Strzembosz. And there were already discussions on legislative reform projects, 20 legislative proposals were prepared. This was then an issue raised in the context of further work. As part of the Round Table's work, it was considered necessary to create... First, it was the idea of this being the Supreme Council of the Court of Justice, but there were comments that the Supreme Council didn't sound very good. And Judge Wasilkowska suggested it was the National Judicial Council. This was absolutely a request on which the party of Solidarity insisted. All guarantees concerning the independence of the National Judicial Council were pressed. This was the response to human rights violations at that time and the lack of independence of the judiciary. The historical background was of great importance here. Probably the most important historical background, such as the document that showed the lack of independence of the judiciary and why it needs to be strengthened, was the Madrid report It's true, isn't it? The report, which was prepared by the KSS and then was the subject of extensive deliberations within the OSCE and which documented the abuse of the right to courts at that time. All these guarantees regarding the non-removalability of Supreme Court judges, the ban on the transfer of judges without their consent and the establishment of a National Judicial Council are in order to respond to the challenges of infringements of the right to court which occurred at that time.

ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-13-senat-52-2.u153	Mrs. Marshal! High Senate! The Committee on Human Rights, Rule of Law and Petitions considered a draft law on the Supreme Court at its meeting. At the meeting, the project was submitted by the representative of the applicant, the office of the President of the Republic of Poland. The opinions of the Legislative Office and the opinions of invited NGOs and central government bodies, the Ombudsman, the National Judicial Council, the Prosecutor General, the Association of Judges stuitia and the Helsinki Foundation for Human Rights were presented. In addition, there were individuals invited by senators. In the discussion and in these opinion-making trends, there were mainly criticisms of this law, because these positive aspects of assumptions and individual provisions were first, as I said, presented by the Minister representing the office of the President of the Republic of Poland. And in response to this presentation, discussion, exchange of opinions, exchange of critical opinions developed. In these critical speeches there were some major trends. But before I talk about them, let me remind the ladies and gentlemen of the senators, which is actually new to the bill, what are the most important solutions proposed in the new law. First of all, it extends the powers of the Supreme Court in such a way that it adds disciplinary proceedings in the field of law and that it reorganizes the internal structure of the Supreme Court, adding the Disciplinar Chamber and the Extraordinary and Public Affairs Board. This Chamber will deal with disciplinary matters of judges, notaries, prosecutors, lawyers, legal advisers, as well as labour and social security matters concerning Supreme Court judges, as well as matters relating to the transfer of the Supreme Court judges to rest. The Act also grants the President of the Republic of Poland the power to issue by regulation the Rules of Procedure of the Supreme Court. It introduces changes in the procedure for the election and appointment of the first Supreme Court President and Supreme Court Presidents. They are based on the fact that the first President will be appointed by the President out of 5 and not, so far, 2 candidates for this position, that the terms of office of the Presidents will be 5 years old and not, so far, 3 years old, and that the Presidents of the Supreme Court will elect the assemblies of the respective chambers of the Supreme Court and the first President will only be consulted. In addition, the Act extends the catalogue of requirements that must be met by a candidate for the post of Supreme Court Judge by the fact that he must have only Polish citizenship and must be 40 years old. It also reduces the age limit to allow the Supreme Court judge to retire to 65 years of age, with the possibility for the President, twice for a period of 3 years, to perform this function. It introduces to the formations of the Supreme Court on extraordinary complaints of jurors who will be elected by the Senate of the Republic of Poland. It extends the catalogue of disciplinary penalties in such a way that it provides for a penalty for the reduction of basic remuneration and the possibility for the disciplinary court to make a final disciplinary judgment public. It introduces the possibility for the President of the Supreme Court to appoint an extraordinary disciplinary officer to conduct a specific disciplinary action concerning a Supreme Court judge. It introduces an obligation to publish resolutions in the Public Information Bulletin on the Supreme Court's website which have obtained the power of the legal principle. It shall introduce a new emergency appeal in the form of an emergency action against all final decisions of universal and military courts which may be brought within 5 years from the date of the decision's enforcement. It also provides for the appointment by the first President of the Supreme Court of the Ombudsman of the public interest as a participant in proceedings for an emergency complaint. I'm skipping out on minor new regulations. These seemed to be the most important ones, which is why I remind you of them. In the light of this law, I think that the High House will be particularly interested in the following criticisms. These comments were formulated in the opinion of the Legislative Bureau. I know that the senators have had a chance to get to know them. Both opinions and earlier opinions about the National Judicial Council and the Supreme Court are included in this well-known set of legal opinions on the desktops. In addition, other opinions were also put forward, and I will only talk about the critical ones. By the Ombudsman, by the Attorney General, by non-governmental organisations. So in order of what do they come down to? It could be said that the main tenor of reservations concerns the unconstitutionality of solutions or the entire law or some of its provisions. I'm picking the most important critical remarks from this trend, I think. The first critical remark is that excessive powers are given to the President, which go beyond his constitutional prerogatives. For this reason, it is necessary that those powers which go beyond prerogatives require countersignation, i.e. the signature of the Prime Minister or the competent minister, and thus the same acts concerning judges will depend on the executive authority. These powers will therefore be opposed to the principle of three divisions of authorities. They will also reduce the importance of internal decisions of the Supreme Court's internal organs when it comes to issuing internal acts relating to the operation of the Supreme Court, since it will be issued by the President. It's mostly about the rules. Same reservation. In conjunction with the allegation that this is incompatible with the Constitution It concerns the President's agreement to continue to take office by the Supreme Court judge after the age of sixty-five. On that occasion, it was argued that this would constitute a politicalisation of the act of consent to the role of judge, because it would depend on a political body, and would have the potential to adversely affect the obligation of impartiality in the judicial role of the judge. The answer to these allegations was mainly in the statements of the Minister who provided the bill on behalf of the applicant. It was replied that these new powers given by the law, going beyond the strictly defined prerogatives of the President, were defined in the teaching of constitutional law in such a way that they were derivatives and could be regarded as an extension of constitutional prerogatives, so that claim is not justified. The next plea, based on the general idea of non-compliance with the Constitution, concerns this provision, which would allow, in view of reaching the age to justify the transition to retirement, the term of office of the first President of the Supreme Court to be shortened. It was claimed that, according to the Constitution, this is a rigid term of six years, and that the ordinary law cannot shorten it. The counterintelligence based on expert opinion was the claim that first you have to be a full judge, not in a resting state, but in an active state, so that any managerial functions can be performed in the judiciary. If, therefore, a person loses his ability to actively pursue the profession of judge due to the transition from right to right of rest, at the same time loses his ability to carry out managerial functions and this results in an automatic shortening of this term of office. And finally, maybe the last of these most fundamental allegations of constitutional nature. It's about introducing an emergency complaint. It expressed concern that this action would violate the principle of the stability of judgments, because it would enable those judgments which already have the character of res judicata to be touched, to be judged if that action was subsequently recognised and re-examined. This plea was answered so that there would be a preliminary review of such a complaint that not everyone who would apply for it would eventually be able to bring the case back to life. This is an institution that responds to social demands, so that grossly wrong decisions which have already been passed and validated can be revised by their injustices. In this way there will be a desirable and necessary supplement to emergency measures, which are known to the Polish legislation with regard to final judgments. As far as NGOs are concerned, but first of all about the ombudsman, the National Judicial Council, their criticisms were reduced, among others. to this In addition to the issues that I mentioned earlier; it was the compatibility of these criticisms that the time range of the emergency complaint was too wide and that such a wide range would result in an excessive rise in cases of this type and could not be resolved in due time, it would also result in a huge increase in expenditure and staff handling of such cases. And that should be critical. The rigor of exclusive Polish citizenship, which will be required of the judge, was also criticized. However, it was argued in response that for some services such rigor has long been present and that it is not a new solution in the Polish law system. It was also criticized as an overly strict provision, which concerns the relationship of affinity and kinship of Supreme Court judges, tightening the current rigor, which will cause difficult situations of life in the event of such affinity or kinship between Supreme Court judges. Retroactivity was also criticized, i.e. the possibility of touching final decisions, disciplinary decisions. This was also considered to be the instability of the judicial system. As far as the emergency complaint is concerned, one more critical remark from the authorities I am talking about was that it would be better not to risk an emergency complaint, but to improve the conditions for the implementation of the deletion and to obtain this route. A bit known and wiped The results are similar to those that wish to be obtained in the emergency complaint procedure. Those were the main critical trends I had chosen. I'm not going to talk about all the positive trends and all the polemics that happened. I am sure that the representative of the applicant, the President, will speak, who will present all the assumptions and arguments in favour of this law. I will therefore conclude my report, adding that, ultimately, after a long meeting of the committee The committee voted by a majority to adopt the law without amendments, and this is what it recommends to the High Senate to react to this law. In addition, by Senator Rulewski and Senator Pociej, a minority motion has been filed against the bill. And if this proposal is not supported, the amendments are to be tabled. Thank you, Mr. Marshal. ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-13-senat-52-2.u218	Thank you very much, Marshal. High Izbo, I would like to comment on some of the comments raised during the discussion. The first issue concerns the expiry of the term of office of the President of the European Union. First President of the Supreme Court. It's not exactly like the rules are losing the term of office of the first president. The Act of 8 December this year. indicates only what requirements must be met in order to take up the position of the first President of the Supreme Court and the President of the Supreme Court accordingly. The requirements for the President are set out in Article. Article 12 (1) and Article 12 (1). 15 §2. One of these requirements is to be an active judge. A similar regulation is currently in force in the Supreme Court Act, in Article. 30 §5, in which there is a clear indication that, if a judge has already reached the age of retirement, even if he has obtained permission to continue his duties, he is not in a position to act as President of the Supreme Court. It can therefore be said that those provisions of the Act of 8 December in so far as they allow for the continuation of their duties after obtaining permission to continue to serve as a judge in an active state In this respect, they are more favourable than those resulting from the current law. As regards the age of retirement, this is largely a reference to the changes in the law on the system of common courts introduced in July this year, which entered into force on 1 October. And now what's different about the resolution of the Law of 8 December 2017 from the law on the system of universal courts is, above all, the regulation concerning women. The Supreme Court Act establishes a uniform age of retirement for women judges and men judges Sixty-five years of age. On the other hand, the law on the system of common courts provides for a separate regulation for women, separate for men. According to the law on the system of common courts, a female judge retires at the age of 60, a male at the age of sixty-five. But the important thing is also that art. 69 §1b indicates that it is possible for the Minister of Justice to authorise the continuation of his duties, to continue to rule, and that the conditions set out here, which the Minister should take into account in the event of a possible extension, are quite vague. Of course, the judge must identify himself with a medical certificate confirming that he can continue to serve as a judge. Second point: the Minister of Justice shall act in accordance with Article. 69 §1b, the rational use of judicial staff and the needs arising from the task of individual courts. So again, these are very judgmental conditions, which do not really allow the long-term answer to be clear as to whether it will really be necessary at a given time to extend the possibility for the judge to rule. The decision will only belong to the Minister of Justice. Another issue that has also been raised here is the question of jurors' education. The requirements laid down in the Act of 8 December refer to those arising from the Law on the system of common courts, specifically Chapter 7. Art. 158 §1 as one of the conditions indicates having at least secondary or secondary education. However, it should also be borne in mind that the requirements set out in the Supreme Court Act are certain minimum requirements. So here again, the Senate, making the choice of jury, will judge each candidate individually. If he/she considers that a candidate, even if he/she has a secondary education, has the right qualities to serve as a juror in the Supreme Court, he/she may be elected. On the other hand, if the High Chamber considers that higher requirements need to be met, it can of course choose the person who meets the higher requirements from among the candidates submitted. What is also worth noting, considering the institution of jurors in the Supreme Court, is that normally in common courts jurors rule in such chambers with professional judges that they are able to pass a vote by issuing a settlement. This will not be the case in the case of the Supreme Court, because always in the formation of the judges of the profession have an advantage over the jury. What are jurors for? The jury is to be a certain social voice, a social sense of justice, which is to be reflected in the decision-making process and consideration of what decision is to be made. Another issue raised here concerns the requirement to have only Polish citizenship as a condition for being a Supreme Court judge. Currently, several laws are in force in the legal system, which also introduce this requirement of having only Polish citizenship, e.g. Article 2 75 §1 of the law on the prosecutor's office indicates that the prosecutor may be a person who has only Polish citizenship. Art. The Commission shall adopt implementing acts laying down detailed rules for the implementation of this Regulation in accordance with Regulation (EU) No 182/2011 of the European Parliament and of the Council concerning the European Parliament and of the Council laying down detailed rules for the application of this Regulation. 2 of Art. The 16th Act on the Service of Military Counterintelligence and the Military Intelligence Service indicates that the head of one or the other service may be a person legitimizing only Polish citizenship. Act on the Internal Security Agency and the Intelligence Agency in Article. 44 The requirement to have only Polish citizenship applies to officers. In addition, other provisions provide for a similar requirement for their heads. The Act on the Central Anti-corruption Office also requires only Polish citizenship by officers, as well as by the head of the CBA. With regard to officers, this requirement shall be governed by Article. 48, in the case of the Head of the CBA, that is the requirement under Article. Paragraph 7 1 of the Central Anti-corruption Bureau Act. Therefore, this is not a new requirement for persons who perform different public functions. Why is this requirement introduced? As in the case of those previously appointed by me in various public functions, the requirement to have only one citizenship, Polish citizenship, in the case of Supreme Court judges, serves to highlight those links which connect the judge with the Polish State. The judge shall deliver judgments and all decisions on behalf of the Republic of Poland. In the case of a person who holds such an important office and issues judgments on behalf of the Republic of Poland, it is important to link this legal hub, which is to have only Polish citizenship. Therefore, the President in his project, which was adopted by the Sejm on 8 December, proposed that the Supreme Court judges should have only Polish citizenship. There were also raised arguments concerning this long period during which an emergency complaint could be brought. It is necessary to take into account the reasons for making use of this measure, which is an emergency complaint. One requirement, apart from violating the rule of law or social justice, is the violation of constitutional freedoms and rights. Therefore, this transitional provision reached the point at which the current constitution entered into force, because that is when this catalogue of freedoms and human rights and the citizen, the violation of which may be one of the conditions justifying the use of this measure, which is an emergency complaint, came into force. It was also pointed out that, despite all this, the period foreseen for an emergency complaint, a period of five years, was quite long. However, in the case of measures currently in place in the legal system, such as deletion whether the action for reopening the procedure can be said to be even longer. In the case of the Minister of Justice and the Prosecutor General, as well as in the case of the Ombudsman or the Ombudsman, there are no time limits for bringing an action. On the other hand, the Civil Procedure Code with regard to the application for reopening provides that, within a period of 10 years after the judgment has become final, this exceptional remedy may be used. Therefore, this is not an excessively long time. This is the same period as for other exceptional measures Such a measure is also an emergency complaint, which is already provided for in the current legislation. There was also a question of countersignation by the President of the Council of Ministers of the President's official acts, which will concern judges of the Supreme Court, but no one argued that, with regard to the administrative courts, the powers of the President of the President concerning the creation of these courts, concerning the determination of the number of judges, also concerning the rules of procedure of the Voivodeship administrative courts, were similar. Any President's regulation as an implementing act shall be countersigned by the President of the Council of Ministers. Since the beginning of these rules giving the President the power to determine this organisation, to determine the rules of the provincial administrative courts, these powers have always been exercised with the countersignal of the President of the Council of Ministers, and I am surprised at the outrage that prevails in connection with the President's competence to establish the rules of the Supreme Court, and to believe that this is such an absolute novelty that has never occurred in the system, and that the President of the Council of Ministers will have a very strong influence on this. Such competence was already in force in the legal system under the amended provisions of the Law on the Supreme Court of 1985. These were provisions modified after the President's office was established. At that time, the President also defined by regulation the organisation of the Supreme Court, its rules of procedure. These provisions were, of course, modified after the entry into force of the Supreme Court Act, which is also currently in force. As regards matters relating to the President's consent to continue to take office by a Supreme Court judge after reaching the age to justify the transition to retirement, it is appropriate to refer here to the voices raised in the doctrine of constitutional law indicating that the countersignations are not only exempted from the requirements of those official acts which expressis verbis are mentioned in Article. ParlaMint-PL_2017-12-13-senat-52-2.u226	Thank you very much, Mrs. Marshal. With regard to the first question concerning the vetoed law, the difference applies primarily to the organisation of the Supreme Court itself, because the vetoed law provides essentially for 3 chambers in the Supreme Court: the Chamber of Public Law, the Chamber of Private Law and the Disciplinary Chamber. And that's the basic difference. Another issue which differs from the law currently being vetoed here is, for example, the termination of the service relationship of the Supreme Court judges. This law, which was passed by Parliament in July, contained provisions to immediately exonerate all Supreme Court judges. The bill under review today does not introduce this kind of regulation because it is not possible to put out the term of office of all the judges... not even the term of office, because the judges are irresistible. But it is not possible to transfer all the judges of the Supreme Court to rest mainly because it would result in total paralysis of the work of the Supreme Court. The differences also concern the President's competence with regard to the Supreme Court, in particular with regard to the adoption of the Rules of Procedure of the Supreme Court, as well as other powers relating to the appointment of the first President or Presidents of the Supreme Court. I'll bring it up here. You raised this state during your work in the High House The fact that the rules were not entirely consistent was because one provision indicated that the President would have five candidates for the first President, while the General Assembly regulations indicated that only three candidates would be elected for the first President of the Supreme Court. In addition, these completely new regulations, which were not in the July Act at all, concern mainly: jurors, which was not provided for in the Supreme Court at all; the introduction of an emergency complaint institution as an extraordinary measure by which final judgments of the courts can be challenged. Of course, there are also issues concerning, for example, the transfer of judges to a resting state or in general the requirements that must be met in order to be a judge of the Supreme Court, because here there has been a modification in relation to those solutions which were adopted in the July Act. So that those were the most important changes. Of course, the various modifications also concerned the possibility of setting up an extraordinary disciplinary officer for judges, because the President, in his original submission, predicted that the Ombudsman would be appointed only from among the judges and did not provide for the possibility of a prosecutor to be appointed as an extraordinary spokesman. In the course of parliamentary work, provisions were added to allow the prosecutor to be appointed as an extraordinary spokesman, but only in the case of the most serious crimes. Minister Mucha explained this perfectly at the committee meeting, and hopes that there will be no need for this institution to be used at all. The Minister pointed out that he believed, however, that the state of the judge had a really high level of culture and therefore there would be no need to use... that judges would not commit these most serious crimes and that there would be no need to appoint a disciplinary officer from a group of prosecutors. As for the next question about the necessity or the need for such a centre as the emergency complaint... Senator Staroń, who is present here, also Mr Wojciechowski, has repeatedly raised the need to restore or introduce the measure following the former emergency review. The introduction of this extraordinary measure was also included in the Law and Justice Programme and in the President's electoral programme. Why? In one of her interpellations, the senator presented several cases where people were wronged by the justice system. They have used all, absolutely all the possibilities for bringing about the judicial decisions that have been given in their cases, but still remain in the legal system of judgments which are not in accordance with the Constitution, which violate their freedoms and rights. Therefore, an extraordinary measure is to be such a final last resort. If someone no longer has any other option to take advantage of the measures provided for by law, he may take advantage of this absolutely extraordinary measure, which is an emergency complaint. In addition, the emergency complaint complements a certain gap in the Polish system of protection measures, which is caused by a constitutional complaint, and more specifically by the formation of a constitutional complaint. Constitutional complaint, in accordance with Article. 79 of the Polish Constitution, is entitled in the event of a violation of constitutional freedoms on the way after obtaining a final decision or a final administrative decision. But the subject of the appeal is not a violation of the freedom or the right to decide the court or the public administration, but a normative act which was the basis for court or administrative proceedings. What does the applicant obtain when the Court has decided on a constitutional action in favour of the applicant? He receives a judgment stating that the normative act is incompatible with the Constitution. But really the recognition of the unconstitutionality of this normative act is only the beginning of the path to obtaining a favourable decision for it. All procedural rules, both administrative, civil and criminal procedures, provide in such a case for the possibility to apply for the reopening of proceedings or a complaint for the reopening of proceedings, the task of which is, on the one hand, to eliminate from the legal market a decision which has been issued on the basis of non-constitutional provisions and, on the other hand, to issue a new decision, based on a new legal situation, one which should not cause such infringements. In the case of a constitutional complaint, it must also be borne in mind that there is another defect resulting from how the Constitutional Tribunal can decide when its judgment enters into force. The decisions of the Constitutional Tribunal may be so called. a deferral clause, i.e. the Constitutional Tribunal, may postpone the entry into force of its judgment for up to 18 months. If a person obtains a favourable ruling stating the non-constitutionality of a normative act, but for some well-known reasons, whether due to economics, the budget of the State, or just a certain pragmatism, he will postpone the date of entry into force of his ruling, he will be pleased to have a favourable judgment of the Court, but he will have to wait until the judgment of the Constitutional Tribunal enters into force so that he can run the entire procedure relating to the reopening of proceedings. An emergency complaint allows, in the event of a breach of the judgment of the court of freedom and constitutional rights, to eliminate, first of all, this non-constitutional settlement, i.e. it introduces the possibility which a constitutional complaint raised repeatedly in various statements at the moment does not give. What does that tell you? I ask you, when in the Constitutional Commission of the National Assembly, this is due to the transcripts of the work of this committee, it was pointed out that a measure such as a constitutional complaint had to be introduced, it was feared that there would be a flood of the Constitutional Court with these complaints, especially in cases where they would be entitled to judicial decisions. But for example. in the Federal Republic of Germany, the constitutional complaint makes it possible to challenge the decision which has been given in the case. What are the consequences? If a federal tribunal concludes that a ruling under the applicable law is correctly formulated, i.e. as regards the applicable rules, there has been no infringement, it shall, secondly, begin to examine whether the provisions on the basis of which this decision was based are in line with the Constitution. So, first of all, on the basis of a constitutional complaint, the person concerned may obtain the annulment of a judgment which is wrong, and if the judgment was correct, then the tribunal is only in charge of prosecuting the constitutionality of the rules. In Poland, the situation is completely different. The Constitutional Tribunal does not examine the compatibility with the Constitution of individual decisions. This is not enough if the constitutional action concerns only questioning an individual decision, then the Court of Justice will reject such a constitutional action as inadmissible in a court of law. There is another point that is worth paying attention to when it comes to a constitutional complaint and which has not been raised. A constitutional complaint may be lodged within 3 months of the date of the final decision. From the point of view of the person concerned, it is quite serious to limit the possibility of investigating violations of the Constitution by a ruling based on non-constitutional rules... in the exercise of their rights. As regards matters relating to Article. 34 and changing the content of the vows, yes, it is a change intended, highlighting the importance of constitutional regulations... [[The removal of the Constitution is intended?]] Mr. Senator, there are no constitutional regulations at the moment. [[But they were.]] [[Deputy Head of the Chancellery of the President of the Republic of Poland Paweł Mucha: No, it was not.]] No, it never was. Currently, the court's vow is enshrined in the current provisions of the Supreme Court Act, in the article. 27 §1, and it reads: "I pledge solemnly as judge of the Supreme Court to serve faithfully the Republic of Poland, to uphold the law, to fulfil the duties of the judge conscientiously, to exercise justice in accordance with the law, impartially according to my conscience, to keep the secret legally protected, and to follow the principles of dignity and integrity.' Senator, all the judges and all the asessors in Poland are declaring rota by vowing to uphold the law, but there is no constitutional law. Believe me, the same rotation of vows in respect of this part is in common courts, in administrative courts, in assesors and in common courts, and in administrative courts, and the same is true in the case of the Supreme Court Act. Therefore, there has never been a reference to the constitution here. Thank you very much.

As regards issues concerning the Polish Citizenship Act... Act on Polish Citizenship in Art. 3 Collision issues are dealt with in the case of persons who have several citizenships. This article indicates that before the Polish authorities, a person who has multiple citizenship cannot rely on these other citizenships because he is treated as a Polish citizen. I can point out to the senator an example we once encountered at the law firm. It turned out that the person who was appointed as ambassador has 2 citizenships of the sending State, as well as Polish citizenship. And there was a problem how this person, who is an ambassador, is to be treated in Poland. According to Art. 3 such a person is treated as a Polish citizen, because all persons who have Polish citizenship, whether or not they have other One nationality or more They will always be treated as Polish citizens. Therefore, if such a person wishes to be subject to diplomatic law and enjoy diplomatic privileges, he must renounce the nationality of the host State so that this collision does not occur. Therefore, in other countries too, this requirement is sometimes formulated for certain authorities. And that's why it's not new, even under the laws of other countries. It is not enough that, in various types of constitutional law studies, it is stressed that at the time of appointment as President of the Supreme Court or the first President of the Supreme Court, this person must be an active judge and the legislator determines the conditions to be met in order to continue this function. Therefore, there is no shortening of the term of office, which results directly from the laws, here is simply an indication of the conditions which must be met by law in order to take over the position of President or First President.
